Kate was torn between finding Kingpin or going to investigate herself. It could be nothing. Kate would have bothered Kingpin for nothing and he’d probably punish her for wasting his time.

    Kate steeled herself before looking for a way up to the roof. She ran into a member of staff outside who offered her yet another drink. Kate shook her head but pointed her finger up at the roof. The waiter looked at her confused for a moment, glancing between her finger and where she was pointing. Kate cocked her head to the side and jabbed a finger at the roof once again.

    “If you want to go to the roof,” the man started slow and unsurely, “then there is a passage in the servant's quarters. Goes straight up.”

    Kate inclined her head in gratitude before she entered the manor once more. Her eyes gazed over the sea of people until she caught sight of Maya before she went to hunt down the servant’s quarters. It was tucked away out of sight of guests and Kate had to roam a few desolate hallways before she found it. She entered and searched for a second until she found the winding spiral staircase that took her up.

    The top of the stairs held a hatch that opened up to the roof. Kate popped a new candy in her mouth before carefully pushing the door open and extracting herself onto the roof. The section was on a slope so Kate carefully replaced the hatch and shuffled along the side until she got to a flat area.

    It was there she saw him peering through the stained glass skylight into the party below. He hadn’t heard her yet, his eyes fixed on the crowd. A sniper rifle was in his hands, and he was using the scope to peer below through a clear portion of the glass.

    Kate took a deep breath. Kingpin told her to get him if unexpected guests showed up. She was to go to his side if Daredevil showed up. The man aiming a gun below was definitely an unwanted guest. But Kate froze in indecision because he seemed focused and she could see that the gun definitely had a magazine in it.

    If Kate left to get Kingpin, she risked that the man would shoot his shot and kill someone. She was unsure of who he was there for. She thought of Maya in the crowds below. Her stomach twisted even more. She rolled the Gobstopper around in her mouth as she thought.

    She eventually decided that she had to at least stop him from shooting anyone. Kingpin would be pissed at her, but he’d be alive to do it.

    Did she want him alive? Did she dare risk his death? Was it worth it? Was he worth saving?

    Kate’s fingers found the handle of the blade strapped to her thigh. If she had her bow, she could have already shot him. She didn’t understand why Kingpin didn’t give one to her. Was he hoping to show her off later, like he had done before? She watched as the man adjusted the gun to look at a different part of the crowd and Kate finally stepped out.

    He whipped his attention to her, the gun following his line of sight. Kate could then see the skull symbol plastered on his chest under the coat he wore, but it wasn’t from a faction she recognized.

    “Jesus fuck, it’s a kid playing dress up,” the man grumbled in a gravely voice.

    Kate stuck her chin up. She couldn’t speak. Wouldn’t dare disobey Kingpin. But she had to get the man away and had to do it quickly. She stepped forward and the man adjusted the grip on his gun.

    “Go home, kid,” he warned dangerously. He squinted at her to make out her form in the moonlight. He watched her for a few seconds at an impasse. “You’re Kingpin’s latest project. Arrow girl.”

    Kate took another step forward.

    “How old are you to get stuck working for a fuck like that?” the man inquired. His finger wasn’t on the trigger, Kate clocked. He was hesitant. Good.

    Kate rolled the candy around in her mouth, scraping the shell along her teeth as she watched.

    “Don’t make me shoot you,” the man finally said. He took a step forward when Kate did, closing the distance between them a little more. “I don’t like killing kids.”

    Kate did her best to keep her face schooled as she moved forward another step.

    “What, you don’t speak?” he inquired sarcastically, something searching in his gaze as he sized her up.

    Kate bit down on the candy, flooding her mouth with tartness before she lunged. The man threw his gun to the side and pulled his own blade as Kate yanked hers from its sheath.

    The man was tall and muscular. He knew how to throw his weight around against someone smaller than him. But Kate had been training with Calvin to take down men just like him.

    The man was wearing armor thick enough to not let a knife penetrate. Kate’s swipe that caught his side did nothing more than tear the fabric to expose the armor.

    The man seemed to mostly be trying to get Kate down instead of actually hurting her. He kept trying to sweep her legs out from under her or spin her until she lost her balance and he could knock her on her back.

    Kate went in for the kill. The man seemed to realize this too, his hand capturing her wrist in a bruising grip, twisting to try and get her to drop the knife.

    “Fucking—give up!” he demanded. Kate used his grip on her to leverage her body weight, swinging her leg up to smash her boot against his nose. His head snapped back, red gushing from his face. He turned to spit out blood that got in his mouth. “You shouldn’t have done that.”

    Kate had been slowly guiding him closer and closer to the edge of the roof. She knew it was dangerous, she could get thrown off, but people could survive falling from three stories up.

    Right?

    Kate focused her attention solely on the man in front of her until he grabbed a fistful of her jacket. Kate yanked her body weight back, slackening her torso as she slid out of the jacket with a quick wiggle. The change in weight made the man stumble back a few feet.

    She didn’t let him get a chance to collect himself. She lunged forward and kicked her leg out, planting her boot onto his chest as she shoved.

    The man tumbled over the edge, his fingers finding purchase on the lip. He went to push himself up but Kate closed the distance once more.

    “You really gonna risk killin’ me?” the man rasped as Kate shoved her boot onto his head, resting it there to stop him from climbing up.

    Kate kicked. The man lost his grip and went down over the edge of the roof. Kate didn’t wait to hear him hit the ground. She snagged the knife he dropped, sheathed her own, and then ran for the hatch to go back down. She slid down the banister of the stairs to get down quicker, stumbling when she jumped off at the bottom.

    Her main focus was finding Kingpin. He was a little harder to find in the crowd than she expected but she eventually located him with a few faction leaders. Kate made her way toward him, bumping into people in the crowd until she skidded to a stop in front of him.

    His eyes fell onto her, his polite smile tightening just a smidge as he politely excused himself from the group before gripping the back of Kate’s neck to steer her into a secluded hallway.

    “Speak,” he ordered. “Where is your jacket?”

    Kate opened her mouth and recalled the events, holding up the knife as proof. Kingpin eventually held his hand up and Kate went silent. Kingpin pulled his phone out and ordered for it to be taken care of. He told whoever was on the other end to find Kate’s jacket and bring it to him.

    When he hung up, his attention turned to her and Kate felt very small under his disapproving gaze.

    “What did I tell you to do if there were unwelcome guests?” he asked in a low tone.

    “Go to you,” Kate said, her voice barely above a whisper.

    “And what did you do?” he said.

    “I disobeyed,” Kate said quietly.

    “You disobeyed!” he agreed, his voice contained just enough to not alert anyone else. “Instead you found it prudent to fight the Punisher of all people. If you were anyone else he would have killed you.”

    His finger jabbed at her chest with the last word and Kate stumbled back at the force. “Punisher?” she echoed.

    Kingpin’s face twisted in displeasure. They were interrupted by a man in a suit arriving, Kate’s purple jacket neatly folded in his grasp.

    “There’s no sign of him,” the man informed Kingpin as they offered out the jacket. Kingpin took it and tossed it at Kate who hurriedly worked it back onto her frame. “We did find evidence on the roof.”

    “Take care of it,” Kingpin ordered and the man nodded before leaving. Kingpin then turned to Kate. “We’ll speak later. For now, you’re the main event.”

    Kate shoved another candy into her mouth as Kingpin steered her back onto the floor. She was Blind- Shot in the spotlight. Kingpin’s latest pet project. He showed her off as eyes fell onto her, announcing why she was his and why she was good.

    Kingpin held out the blindfold for all to see. Kate shoved her worries away because she knew what to do as Blind-Shot. She knew how to shoot. She could do it blindfolded. She let Kingpin tie the fabric around her eyes and held her hands out for her bow.

    Whenever the fabric went over her eyes, the rest of Kate’s senses felt heightened. The room around her, although quiet, was still loud. She could hear people shuffling in place, sips from flutes of champagne, coughing, and hushed whispers.

    She waited for the whistle. She aimed her bow when she heard it only to freeze when another whistle came from a different direction. Her head snapped in the direction of the second whistle, confusion filling her. Which one was her target?

    The first one whistled again. Kate steadied her aim. The second whistle came. Kate ignored it. She took a deep breath before letting the arrow sail, pulling out a new one and hooking it in her bow to shoot at the second target.

    Polite applause filled the room and relief filled Kate. Focus on two targets was something she had not practiced with Calvin or her archery instructor.

    Kate let herself get lost in her head as Kingpin showed her off. She let the night turn into a smear of colors and people, glad that she didn’t have to speak or respond and could just nod or bow her head in acknowledgment.

    She found herself by the bar, watching the bartender mix fancy and complicated drinks that were demanded. A hand fell on her shoulder and she jumped, spinning to look at the culprit.

    “Hello, my dear,” Madam Red greeted her. Kate let herself relax as Madame Red pressed a kiss to the side of her head in greeting. With her lips closer to her ear, Madame Red whispered, “It was you who caused such a fuss earlier, wasn’t it?”

    Kate tried to keep herself calm. She didn’t answer but luckily Madame Red didn’t chastise her for that.

    “Has Fisk seen fit to punish you yet?” Madame Red inquired in a murmur as she pulled away. Kate shook her head, watching the fleeting expression flash over Madame Red’s face.

    Madame Red raised a finger and called the bartender over. Kate wasn’t familiar with the drink she ordered and was surprised when Madame Red slid the glass in front of her once it was made.

    “Have a soother,” Madame Red muttered. Kate reluctantly picked up the glass, taking a tentative sniff at the drink. It was fruity smelling. “You’re going to need it.”

    Kate pressed the glass to her lips and took a sip. It was heavy on the alcohol and it burned going down, the aftertaste of cranberries and oranges lingering on her tongue. Madame Red seemed content to sit next to her and nurse her own drink, creating a bubble that caused the others to leave them alone.

    “You shoot magnificently,” Madame Red commented. She ordered another drink when Kate finished hers, sliding a second in front of her. “You’re a very talented archer. I can already tell you’re going to do brilliant things. Horrible, but brilliant.”

    Kate busied herself by picking up the second glass. She didn’t want to think about it. Kingpin gave her the opportunity to refine her skills, one she probably never would have gotten in a normal lifetime. She was grateful, but the cost of her skills going toward something so wrong was the only thing holding her back.

    Kate rejected the third glass ordered. She knew better than to let Madame Red get her past tipsy. She didn’t want to think about the last time she was drunk with Madame Red.

    Maya found her at the bar and eyed Madame Red. Kate was already standing to slide between them, bowing her head in gratitude at Madame Red as she gently took Maya by the arm to steer her back into the crowd of people.

    Maya asked where she had been. Said she hadn’t seen Kate very much at all. Kate told her that she did rounds like she said. She did not mention the Punisher. There was no need to make Maya upset.

    Maya tugged Kate out to dance with the others and Kate reluctantly let Maya pull her close to dance as a pair. Kate had enough formal dance training with all of the charity auctions she attended with her mom when she was younger.

    While Kate was rigid and stiff in her dancing, Maya was much looser, grabbing at Kate’s hands to tug at them and trying to loosen her up, tripping up her rhythm and making Kate constantly have to adjust. Kate would be annoyed if it wasn’t for the teasing grin on Maya’s face.

    Maya reached her right hand up with her thumb and pointer finger splayed, pressing it against her forehead. Loser.

    Kate wrinkled her nose at the challenge. Maya laughed as Kate let herself go offbeat and spun her around. The moment was short-lived and Kate immediately stopped when she noticed a few stares, but she let Maya continue to lead her around the dance floor.

    Kate did two more rounds before the night ended in the wee hours of the morning. It wasn’t until the manor was nearly empty did Kingpin find her and Maya settled at the bar with nonalcoholic drinks.

    “Maya,” Kingpin greeted his niece first, a soft smile gracing his face. Maya smiled back at him, waving hello. “Why don’t you go on ahead? I’ll drop Kate off when we’re done.”

    Maya glanced at Kate quickly in confusion before signing to Kingpin. The large man raised an eyebrow and Kate suddenly remembered that Kazi usually did all of the translation between them. She would have thought that with how long Kingpin had known her, he would have at least picked up on some sign language for Maya.

    “She says that William is waiting for both of us to come back at the same time,” Kate roughly translated.

    Kingpin’s smile lost its edge slightly before he nodded. “Very well. You may wait here while Kate and I take a lap around the manor to speak.”

    Maya didn’t get a chance to protest before Kingpin was guiding Kate out the back into the courtyard.

    Kingpin stopped before he grabbed the front of her jacket, bending down as he yanked Kate closer to his face.

    “Tonight could have turned out a wreck if you failed,” he said in a low tone. “You are a foolish and naive girl for thinking you could face him. It is merely because of your age that he let you go with your life. He was testing you to see if you were capable of death and now that he knows, you will no longer be spared.”

    Kate could feel her heart pounding in her chest but the alcohol Madame Red gave to her kept her relatively calm.

    “I’m sorry, sir,” Kate said quietly.

    “That being said.” His grip loosened, and his hands instead moved to frame her face, his touch gentle and delicate. “You prevented what could have been a tragedy. You protected us like you should have, and for that, you have made me proud.”

    If Kate wasn’t wearing a mask, she was sure Kingpin would laugh at the surprise on her face. “I made the right call?” she asked unsurely.

    Kingpin patted her cheek. “You did well tonight, my little archer.”

    Kate thought he was going to punish her. She expected a rough shake at the least, maybe a harsh smack to remind her of the rule she broke. But being told he was proud of her was the last thing she expected.

    She wasn’t sure if it was the alcohol that made her feel warm or his words.

    “You have the day off tomorrow.” Kingpin pulled his hands away and straightened up. “You’ve earned it. Keep up the excellent work, Kate. I know you’ll continue to please me.”

    Kate straightened up slightly at his words. “I will,” she promised.

    Kingpin clasped her shoulder before he continued to walk. They made a loop around the manor in silence, his hand weighing on her shoulder. When they met up with Maya again, she quickly gave Kate a once-over before they parted ways from Kingpin.

    Maya pressed up against her in the backseat of the car. Kate was too exhausted to question it, running her fingers through her hair that was starting to come undone from the gel she had applied hours earlier. She wasn’t sure when but she fell asleep, her head pillowed on Maya’s shoulder as the alcohol coupled with the hum of the car engine lulled her.

    Maya woke her up when they arrived back. Kate groggily followed her out of the car and up the steps to the house. Maya reached out to knock on the door, her arm falling over Kate’s shoulder as she swayed from her exhaustion.

    It felt like forever but the door eventually opened and they were greeted by the sight of William. It looked like he hadn’t even tried to go to bed yet, still in his uniform.

    His eyes swept over them before standing aside to let them in. As Kate bent down to undo her boots, William asked how things went.

    “Fine,” Kate said, kicking her boot off when it was loose enough.

    “Were you both safe?” he inquired.

    Kate paused from where she was undoing her other boot before she straightened and turned to look at him. “As safe as we could be in a room with other faction leaders.”

    William scrutinized her for injuries but he would find nothing visible. Kate yawned and he relented on his interrogation, letting her toe her other boot off.

    Kate washed the gel out of her hair in the sink, dissolving the glue on her mask and peeling it from her face. As she was changing out of her uniform, she got a good look at the wrist that Punisher had gripped and twisted to get her to try and drop the knife.

    A reddish-purple mark was forming. There was no way Kate was going to be able to hide it unless she wore long sleeves. She glanced at the short-sleeved sleep shirt she grabbed before tugging it on. It was inevitable that she would be found out.

    Kate left the bathroom in her pajamas, securing the uniform with her bag of clothes. Maya was already in her bedroom but William was still up and in the dark living room, watching something on the television that illuminated the room in a blue hue, and nursing a mug of coffee. He glanced at her when she hesitantly stepped into his field of view.

    “I lied,” she stated. William set his mug down, his attention solely on her. “Maya doesn’t know but I got into a fight. I have a bruise on my wrist but that’s it. I’m okay.”

    “Let me see,” William ordered. Kate approached him and held her wrist out. He took it and guided her hand under the light coming from the television to survey the damage. He made her flex her hand and wiggle her fingers to prove there was no damage before he told her to ice it.

    Before Kate pulled away, William clasped her hand between his. “Thank you for telling me, Kate.”

    Kate hesitated before asking, “Are you mad?”

    William shook his head. “You came home to me. That is all that matters.”

    Kate wasn’t sure what to do with the feelings bubbling in her chest. As she pulled a bag of frozen vegetables from the freezer, she tried to convince herself it was the alcohol. It wasn’t until she had wrapped the bag in a towel that she realized William was still in the living room where she slept. He didn’t seem like he’d be going to bed anytime soon and Kate feared if she went back out there and asked then there would be an interrogation about what exactly happened.

    Still, Kate was exhausted, so her feet took her to the place where Maya said she was always welcome.

    Maya was already in bed under the covers with the lights off. Kate carefully padded into the room and stood at the edge of the bed. Maya must have either not been fully asleep or sensed her because she rolled over to make room without even looking at her. Kate slid into bed, sinking into the mattress with a sigh of relief as she rested the frozen bag of vegetables on her wrist.

    It took no time at all before she fell asleep.

Kate almost felt like his kid sometimes. He knew every dark part about her and embraced her the way that she was. Kate’s body had an internal alarm clock that woke her up. Maya had this way of curling into a little ball in her sleep, her dark hair spilling over her pillow like ink across a page.

    Kate shuffled out of the bedroom and down the hall before she paused, her mind blurry and tangled in the fog of waking up. Kingpin told her the previous night that she had the day off, didn’t he? The memory was hazy. Had she imagined it? The headache in her skull throbbed, and her stomach twisted with a dull nausea. Kate rubbed at her eyes and let out a frustrated sigh, unable to shake the dull ache spreading from her temples. Maybe it had been a drunken prayer, a hopeful wish whispered in the dark to the night that would never come true.

    Regardless, she figured she’d get ready anyway. If Jonah never showed up to drag her back into the mess, then she could call it a free day. She wasn’t sure what she was waiting for. It wasn't as if she had anywhere to go.

    She shuffled into the kitchen, the scent of stale coffee lingering in the air. Kate’s hand felt like it was moving on its own, reaching for the juice bottle, uncapping it, and pouring herself a glass. She grimaced as she took a sip, feeling it settle in her stomach like lead.

    She could feel the weight of sleep pressing down on her, soft and heavy like a warm fog, pulling her back into its embrace. So, instead of doing anything useful, Kate dragged herself into the living room and collapsed onto the couch, her limbs heavy. She shifted a little, trying to find the right spot, and let her eyes fall closed.

    Before she could stop herself, the soft weight of sleep consumed her again. Time felt fluid in the haze of her exhaustion. It felt like hours passed in an instant. The quiet of the house was soothing, and the slight thrum of her headache became a background hum.

    She swam to consciousness once again, the sound of footsteps awakening her. They were quiet and deliberate, and then they stopped just outside the living room. Kate’s head was still foggy, and she almost thought she was imagining it—the quiet shuffle of feet.

    She wanted to ignore it and just sink back into the quiet embrace of sleep, but the footsteps didn’t leave. They lingered, a presence outside her little world of exhaustion. Kate felt her body tense, a trained instinct that surfaced.

    The footsteps entered, and her mind screamed that she should be alert, but she couldn’t summon the energy. The rustle of fabric brushed against her ears as someone came closer, and before she could blink, a blanket was gently draped over her. A warm hand settled briefly on her shoulder, guiding her into a more comfortable position, shifting her body as though it were second nature. Kate let herself be moved, too tired to protest, too grateful for the comfort of the gesture.

    She was laid down carefully, her legs lifted onto the couch with an ease that surprised her. The blanket was adjusted, tucked in close around her, cocooning her in warmth and safety. The touch was soft, like it was meant to ensure she could slip deeper into rest without a care in the world. And then, the steps retreated, soft and quiet again, leaving her in a stillness that felt almost sacred.

    Kate didn’t even have the energy to open her eyes to see who had done it. Kate let her body relax again, surrendering to the pull of sleep. She didn’t care what time it was. It didn’t matter. For the moment, she could just rest.

    Kate's mind was awash in darkness. The nightmare was still vivid when she woke, her chest heaving with shallow breaths. Sweat clung to her skin, making her feel like she was trapped in the remnants of a storm. Her hands were trembling, her heart pounding against her ribs like it was trying to break free.

    In the nightmare, Madame Red had loomed over her—cold, cruel, and relentless. The woman’s hands had been everywhere, pinning Kate down with a suffocating force, her laughter sharp and cruel as Kate struggled beneath her, helpless. Every time Kate had tried to fight back, Madame Red's grip only tightened, her voice taunting Kate with promises of what was to come. Kate had screamed in the dream, begged, but the sound never came. She had been trapped, alone, and it had felt real.

    Kate’s eyes were wide, her hands clutching the edges of the couch as if it could hold her together. Her breath was jagged, uneven, and her skin felt too tight, the heat of the sweat on her forehead mixing with the cold dread pooling in her gut.

    She didn’t hear the soft footsteps at first. The quietness of the house had made it easy for her to pretend she was still alone. Her vision was blurry from the tears that had started to spill as the nightmare’s echoes began to subside, but in the quiet, something shifted.

    Then, out of nowhere, a hand was on her shoulder, and the softness of it made Kate flinch. Her instinct was to pull away, but something inside her froze in place. She wasn’t alone.

    “Maya?” Kate whispered hoarsely. The warmth from the hand on her shoulder didn’t leave her, and the touch was gentle and grounding.

    Maya’s presence was a quiet one, her movements never intrusive. Kate hadn’t heard her approach, but there was no mistaking her.

    Kate closed her eyes, trying to steady herself, but it felt impossible. The vision of Madame Red’s face lingered in the corners of her mind, and Kate had to fight to keep the panic from overtaking her completely. Her hands balled into fists, and she pressed them to her face, trying to block out the memory of being trapped.

    Maya’s hands moved to Kate’s, gently pulling them away from her face. The touch was firm, but kind, and it was the kind of touch that made Kate feel safe, even in the midst of the chaos her mind was still battling. Maya’s fingers moved over her hands in a familiar, soothing gesture, a language Kate knew well, even if the world didn’t always understand it.

    Kate’s mind filtered through the words she knew as Maya told her to breathe and promised they were safe in quick, fluid movements. Kate’s chest hitched as she tried to process the words, but they pierced through the fog of distress like a lifeline.

    Kate sucked in a breath, shaky at first, and then another. Maya was patient with her, her hand holding Kate’s tightly. Maya never rushed, never asked too much of her.

    Finally, Maya reached for her, pulling Kate gently into her arms, guiding her to rest her head against her shoulder. Kate didn’t pull away.

    The nightmare was still lingering in the back of her mind, but the steady thrum of Maya’s heartbeat against her ear, the quiet, constant comfort of her presence, made it feel a little more distant, a little less overwhelming.

    With Maya’s arms around her, she could breathe again. Slowly, the remnants of the nightmare began to fade, replaced by the warmth of the moment. It wasn’t a solution, not really, but it was enough to keep her from sinking into the darkness again.

    Maya didn’t ask about the nightmare, didn’t press her for answers. She just held her.

    It was a strange kind of comfort, knowing someone cared enough to stay, to hold her when she needed it most.

    With her free day, Kate spent a lot of time thinking. She tucked her knees against her chest as she stared at the wall in front of her in the living room. There was some moving playing on the television, and Maya was stretched out on the couch next to her watching it. There was a finished bowl of popcorn between them and Kate’s fingers were still greasy with butter.

    Despite her best efforts, Kate’s thoughts kept drifting to her mom. She always did after terrible nightmares.

    They hadn’t always had the easiest relationship. In fact, most of their conversations had been riddled with tension, words unsaid, anger buried under layers of distance. Her mom had always tried to shield Kate from the world that Kate had ended up in, even as she herself was swept deeper into it. She had tried, in her own flawed way, to protect Kate from the darkness, but it hadn’t been enough. And Kate, for her part, hadn’t made it easy.

    Kate was left wondering what her mom would say if she saw her. Would she be proud of her, somehow? Proud that her daughter had survived so long in a world so brutal, so unforgiving? Maybe. But more likely, her mom would be horrified by what Kate had become, by the things she’d done. The blood on her hands, the lives she had taken—those were the things her mom would see, the things that would cloud her love. Would she have condemned her? Would she have turned away in disgust, unable to reconcile the person Kate had become with the child she once was?

    Kate’s stomach twisted at the thought.

    She could almost hear her mom's voice, soft and strained, calling her name in the way only a mom could. Kate had always taken that voice for granted, even when they fought. Even when her mother’s words were sharp, full of disappointment, Kate had never really understood how much they meant until she no longer had the chance to hear them.

    Would her mom hold her, despite everything? Would she let go of the pain and the disappointment and just hold her in a way that made Kate feel like a child again, safe, even if just for a moment?

    She had found the pictures in her mom’s safe. She wasn’t exempt from the violence she had to commit. There was blood on her hands too, even if it wasn’t literal.

    Maya’s father had never condemned her for what she had to do. Maya had killed people before. Not a lot by the sounds of it, Kate probably had a higher body count at that point, but it was still the same.

    William handled Kate with a kindness she wasn’t sure her own mom would extend. His worn hands would handle her gently as he asked after her health and safety. Kate almost felt like his kid sometimes. He knew every dark part about her and embraced her the way that she was. She never felt ashamed of what she had to do with him. If she had to take a life, William would offer to be there to watch her back.

    “Fine,” Kate mumbled, her hands coming up to sign as she spoke. “Just… thinking.”

    Kate hesitated before admitting, “My mom.”

    Understanding flashed across Maya’s face. Maya confessed to missing her mom too. Kate sighed. Maya had lost her mom many years ago. She wanted to ask if the craving for a mother’s comfort ever went away, but she didn’t want to upset her.

    Still, she didn’t have anything else to do so she shrugged. “Okay. Why not.”

    In reply, Kazi let out a large burp, and Kate laughed. Maya wrinkled her nose in disgust while Kazi grinned at her.

    “Have you… killed people before?” Clementine asked. The words came out slower, hesitant, as if she already knew the answer and hated herself for asking.

    Kate hesitated. Then nodded. “Yes.”

    Clementine inhaled sharply, then let the breath out in one slow exhale. “Oh.”

    Kate didn’t know what else to say. An awkward silence fell over the car.

    “That’s why Madame is sending you, isn’t it?” Clementine said quietly. “Because you’ve killed before.”

    “I think so,” Kate admitted. “I don’t know. I do what I’m told.”

    Clementine looked away before nodding. “What do I call you?”

    “Blind-Shot.” She wished it didn’t feel like she was setting something down between them. A wall, or a blade. She wished she could still be Tara here. That none of Madame Red’s girls ever had to meet Blind-Shot. Or Fetch. Or even Kate.

    But they had.

    And Clementine deserved the truth.

    “That’s an odd name,” Clementine said, trying to smile as she adjusted her miniskirt. Her fingers brushed across her thighs like she was trying to smooth away the tension. “Why that name?”

    “Because I can shoot a bow and arrow blindfolded,” Kate replied.

    Clementine blinked at her, the surprise genuine.

    Kate gave a small shrug. “I just have a knife on me today. But I prefer bows.”

    “Blind-Shot,” Clementine repeated, like she was tasting the name, weighing its shape on her tongue. Her gaze wandered back to the window. Kate watched the way the rising sun turned the gloss on her lips into something luminous, her skin glowing gold at the edges.

    Clementine was dressed up more than usual—more makeup, heels slightly higher. Her hair curled and soft. She looked like she was going somewhere nice. She wasn’t.

    “How is Blind-Shot different from Tara?” Clementine asked.

    Kate’s voice dropped. “Blind-Shot doesn’t speak.”

    Clementine looked startled, but nodded. “Okay.”

    Kate added quickly, “But I’ll be watching. If you need me, I’ll be there.”

    “I know,” Clementine whispered.

    Kate didn’t know what she expected the house to look like. A mansion, maybe. Something grotesquely lavish, vulgar in its decadence. Or maybe the opposite—a dive with bars on the windows, broken furniture, the stink of something festering beneath the floorboards. Someplace you could tell was wrong just by looking at it.

    But this place looked… ordinary.

    Two stories. Beige siding. Weather-worn, but intact. There were wind chimes on the porch. A doormat that said Home Sweet Home, slightly askew. The kind of house that tried to disappear by being just like the others.

    Clementine got out first, heels clicking softly against the cracked concrete. She walked like she wasn’t shaking. Like the night ahead wasn’t already crawling beneath her skin. Kate followed a step behind, keeping her profile low and her expression unreadable.

    The man who opened the door lit up the moment he saw Clementine.

    “My sweet girl,” he purred, voice thick with grease and rot. “You look delicious tonight.”

    Clementine smiled the way girls like her were taught to—wide, with just enough teeth to be coy, not threatening. She said nothing, just leaned in with a laugh that made Kate’s stomach twist.

    Kate kept her silence. She let her eyes slide past him as she stepped inside.

    The inside of the house was suffocatingly normal.

    Tile floors, beige walls. Faux-leather furniture with a few nicks in the seams. A flat-screen TV sat dark in the corner, forgotten. Five men milled about the living room in shirtsleeves and loosened ties. A bottle of vodka sat already open on the table, along with a few half-drained glasses and a bottle of cheap whiskey that still had its price tag on.

    No food. No music. Just the scrape of glasses, the soft mutter of anticipation. A clock ticked faintly behind a dusty potted plant, tucked almost out of sight.

    Kate’s eyes swept the room—two exits, both unobstructed. No visible cameras, but that didn’t mean they weren’t there. No weapons either. At least not obvious ones. A lamp could break into shards. A belt could bruise. A bottle could do both.

    One of them handed Clementine a drink. She took it, smiled, and downed it like water.

    “Hey, shorty,” one of them called to Kate. “Come loosen up.”

    She shook her head. She didn’t trust the glass, didn’t trust the smile. The man turned away, disinterested. She counted that as a win.

    The vodka bottle was now being passed between three of the men, one of them already starting to slur his words. They hadn’t brought out food—only drinks and whatever sick expectations they carried in their teeth. Kate’s grip tightened slightly on the edge of her coat.

    And then Clementine’s top came off.

    Kate held still. Fists clenched in fabric, jaw locked. Her chest ached with the effort not to move. She was warned—no lasting marks. Bruising was fine. That was the rule. That was always the rule.

    The skirt went next.

    Kate looked away, not in shame, but in calculation. There was no saving Clementine from what was coming. Her job wasn’t to protect her from pain. It was to make sure it didn’t go too far.

    She forced herself to breathe.

    She started counting the letters on the vodka bottle. Over and over. Memorizing the pattern in the label’s font. The crest on the cigarettes left out on the table. She spelled the words backward in her head, again and again, until the grunts and gasps blurred into static behind her thoughts.

    And finally, after a long while, the men decided they needed a break. Clementine was left draped over a coffee table like a discarded doll, flesh red and bruised, skin slick with sweat. Kate approached her as the men went about their own business.

    Kate helped Clementine sit up and took stock of the marks. It looked a little painful, and Clementine was definitely going to be feeling it tomorrow.

    “I’m okay,” Clementine whispered hoarsely.

    Kate left Clementine briefly to head to the kitchen. One of the men was there, raising an eyebrow at her and asking “What the fuck do you want?”

    “Water,” Kate grunted at him. The man gave a dismissive wave at the fridge, and Kate approached it, finding a few sealed bottles in the door and selecting one. She returned to the sitting room where Clementine was and cracked open the bottle before offering it to her.

    “Do you see a clock?” Clementine asked hoarsely.

    Kate’s eyes scanned the room until she found one tucked out of view and said quietly, “Nine hours in.”

    Clementine took a few deep breaths, closing her eyes and nodding. “Almost halfway there.”

    Kate crouched in front of her again, watching the small tremors that rippled through Clementine’s limbs. Her lipstick had bled at the corners, mascara smudged to a raccoon halo around her eyes, and her breath came unevenly quiet, but pulled from somewhere deeper than exhaustion.

    “Do you want to lie down?” Kate asked softly.

    Clementine blinked slowly, the question seeming to take a second to reach her. Then she shook her head.

    Kate nodded once, then shifted on her feet. “Do you want to sit up more?” she tried again.

    “I don’t know,” Clementine murmured. Her voice cracked halfway through. “I just… stay with me?”

    Kate stepped around the coffee table and eased herself onto it, one knee up, one leg dangling. The wood groaned under her weight, but she didn’t care. She carefully scooted until she was straddling the space directly behind Clementine and gently tugged her upright by the forearms. Clementine moved with her, pliable, her body slow and trembling, until she was seated and leaning back against her.

    Clementine’s bare shoulders met the rough fabric of Kate’s shirt and jacket, the warmth from her skin immediately seeping through. Kate kept her hands still in her lap at first, unsure of where to put them, unsure what Clementine needed. But when Clementine let out a tiny, ragged sigh and slumped back against her like a doll folding in on itself, Kate took the cue and raised one arm to rest lightly across her middle, mindful of the bare skin and marks.

    She could feel the jump in Clementine’s breath.

    “Sorry,” Clementine mumbled. “I’m gonna get your clothes all gross.”

    “So what?” Kate mumbled.

    Clementine closed her eyes again, lashes clumped together in thick black smears. Her breathing slowed in increments—still shaky, but no longer gasping. Just enough for Kate to feel it, soft puffs brushing through the fabric near her collarbone.

    The room was dim except for the kitchen light bleeding in around the corner. One of the men was muttering to himself while rifling through a cabinet, and the others had retreated to the back room. Kate could still hear them talking, laughing, the occasional sharp clang of glass against wood, but for the moment, none of them were paying attention. That’s what mattered.

    “Sorry you have to watch,” Clementine whispered.

    Kate didn’t answer at first. Then, “It is what it is.”

    They sat like that until the men returned. One of them leered at the scene. “What’s this? You joining in, doll?”

    Kate’s head snapped up. Her glare was silent and scalding. She carefully eased Clementine upright to slip out from behind her. Her leg was numb from holding the weight, and she stumbled for just a second before forcing herself to walk on pins and needles back to the wall where she stood like a sentinel.

    She slipped a piece of candy from her pocket and popped it into her mouth.

    Sweetness. Focus. Something to hold onto.

    And she waited.

    Finally, after far too long, the time ended.

    Kate helped Clementine sit upright, careful not to jostle her too quickly. The girl moved like she’d been half-stitched back together, her body slow to respond, muscles trembling beneath clammy skin. Her makeup was ruined, a smeared constellation of glitter and mascara framing her hollowed-out eyes. She winced when Kate steadied her by the arms.

    “It’s over,” Kate murmured. She didn’t know if it was meant to comfort Clementine or herself.

    Clementine gave a faint nod, but her gaze was distant, tracking something in the air that wasn’t there. Her voice, when it came, was rough-edged and almost slurred. “Did I… do okay?”

    Kate's hands tightened slightly around her arms.

    “Yeah, you did fine.” It was the only truth Kate had.

    The sound of a car rolling up the gravel driveway cut through the early morning hush like a blade. Tires ground to a stop. Doors slammed. Footsteps—sharp, assured. The front door creaked open, and the air in the room shifted.

    Madame Red stepped inside.

    She didn’t knock. She didn’t announce herself. She simply arrived. Her presence filled the space, elegant and surgical. The men quieted. Even the drunk ones sobered slightly at the sight of her. Her perfume floated into the room like a warning.

    “Well,” she said, her voice velvet and steel as she crossed the room to them, “how are my girls?”

    Kate said nothing.

    Clementine blinked up at her slowly, still folded against the couch. “I’m okay,” she rasped.

    Madame Red tilted her head and looked her over like a buyer inspecting damaged goods. Her expression was unreadable, but her eyes flicked across every visible bruise, every mark, every slip of smeared makeup.

    “You’ve done well,” she said finally, smoothing a stray curl from Clementine’s face. “You’ll rest now.”

    She turned her attention to Kate.

    “And you,” she said. “No weapons drawn. No interference.”

    Kate straightened. “Didn’t have to.”

    Madame Red offered a cool smile. “That’s what I like to hear.”

    She didn’t ask if Clementine needed help walking. She didn’t reach out. She simply turned toward the door, assuming—correctly—that Kate would handle it.

    Kate got Clementine to her feet with gentle tugs and bracing arms. Clementine wobbled but stayed upright, leaning heavily into her side. She didn’t protest. She didn’t pretend to be stronger than she was. She let herself be held.

    As they passed the table, one of the men lifted his glass lazily and said, “Tell Madame I’m always happy to donate to the cause.”

    Kate didn’t look at him. She didn’t trust herself to.

    Madame Red paused in the doorway just long enough to offer a glance over her shoulder.

    “I’m sure you are,” she said smoothly, before walking out into the morning chill.

    Kate picked up the discarded clothes on the floor, helped Clementine manipulate her limbs through the correct holes, and then guided her out the door toward the waiting car.

    Kate used the showers at Madame Red’s. She felt disgusting, like she had the scent of alcohol, cigarettes, and sex clinging to her. She didn’t have the foresight to bring a change of clothes, which was a mistake on her part.

    She nearly fell asleep in the shower under the warm water. She was exhausted. The caffeine pills could only do so much.

    Kate borrowed a change of clothes, and Madame Red got her driver to give Kate a ride home.

    The car ride was quiet. The driver didn’t speak. Kate didn’t either.

    She stared out the window, watching the city blur past in pale gray streaks. Her body throbbed with fatigue. Her fingers twitched once when she almost nodded off, and she forced herself to sit up straighter. She wouldn’t sleep in a stranger’s car. She couldn’t afford that kind of vulnerability.

    She unlocked the door to the Lopez home with one hand, the other cradling her folded uniform and mask. The entryway light was off. The house was quiet, still heavy with the hush of early morning. She stepped inside and shut the door behind her with a soft click, slipping off her shoes without making a sound.

    She made it to her room in a straight line.

    Her arms felt like dead weight by the time she dropped the uniform into the laundry basket and peeled the borrowed shirt off her back. She changed into her softest pajamas—worn cotton, a size too big— and let the fabric settle over her skin. The mask went into the drawer. She closed it a little too hard.

    She moved to the kitchen next, still barefoot. Opened the fridge. Stared at the contents for a moment longer than necessary.

    She made toast. Something simple. Something she could watch brown in real time. She poured herself a glass of water and drank it too fast, then made another piece of toast and added honey. Just for the sweetness.

    She ate in silence at the counter, head bowed, legs curled up on the stool like a child. There was no appetite, only the knowledge that she should. She needed something in her stomach that hadn’t been handed to her by someone with an ulterior motive. Something that wasn’t laced or spiked or wrapped in expectation.

    Once she finished, she just stood there, hands gripping the edge of the countertop, staring down at the empty plate, breathing. Letting the stillness settle in her bones.

    When she finally made her way to Maya’s room, the floor felt like it shifted beneath her, like the world had gone uneven while she wasn’t paying attention.

    Kate didn’t knock. She didn’t need to.

    She opened the door with the quiet confidence of someone who had been welcomed before and stepped inside. The room was dark, save for the faint sliver of light bleeding through a crack in the curtains.

    She moved toward the bed and hesitated. Not because she doubted. But because the act of reaching out, of asking for comfort, still didn’t come naturally.

    She curled her fingers into the blanket at the foot of the bed and gave it a soft tug.

    Maya stirred immediately. She blinked the sleep from her eyes and rolled over, eyes squinting against the dark. The moment she saw Kate, she lifted the blanket without a word.

    Invitation. No questions. No judgment.

    Kate crawled in slowly, her limbs aching, her muscles stiff. She settled beneath the covers and felt them tuck in around her like a cocoon. Maya reached up without hesitation and brushed a thumb across her cheek, catching a bead of water that had rolled down from Kate’s damp hair. Not a tear. Just leftover from the shower. But the touch still made her throat close up.

    <You okay?> Maya signed, fingers slow and careful in the low light.

    Kate blinked once. Her body felt like it was beginning to short-circuit now that it was safe enough to relax.

    <Tired,> she signed back. The word was simple, but the weight behind it wasn’t.

    Maya’s response was a soft, raspy hum, barely a sound. Her fingers brushed lightly along Kate’s shoulder as she signed, <Sleep.>

    Kate didn’t argue. Couldn’t.

    She let her eyes slip shut, her body folding closer into the warm dip of the mattress, into Maya’s scent —jasmine, and something that always reminded her of cedarwood and sun. The blankets shifted as Maya tugged them higher, covering them both.

    Kate tucked her face against Maya’s shoulder and let herself breathe in time with her.

    She slept.
